Running coupling constant in thermal ϕ 4 \phi^{4} theory up to two loop order
K. Arjun, A. M. Vinodkumar, Vishnu Mayya Bannur

Abstract
Using the imaginary time formalism in thermal field theory, we derive running coupling constant and running mass in two loop order. In the process, we express the imaginary time formalism of Feynman diagrams as the summation of non-thermal quantum field theory (QFT) Feynman diagrams with coefficients that depend on temperature and mass. Renormalization constants for thermal ϕ 4 \phi^{4} theory were derived using simple diagrammatic analysis. Our model links the non-thermal QFT and the imaginary time formalism by assuming both have the same mass scale μ \mu and coupling constant g . When these results are combined with the renormalization group equations (RGE) and applied simultaneously to thermal and non-thermal proper vertex functions, coupling constant and running mass with implicit temperature dependence are obtained. We evaluated pressure for scalar particles in two loop orders at zero external momentum limit by substituting the running mass result in the quasi-particle model.



pacs
Valid PACS appear here

I
Introduction

There are different quasi-particle models
Goloviznin and Satz 1993
;
Peshier et al. 1994
;
Schneider and Weise 2001
;
Meisinger et al. 2004
;
Bannur 2007a
;
Bannur 2007b
;
Bannur 2007c
;
Koothottil and Bannur 2019
;
Koothottil and Bannur 2020
used to describe the state of the quark-gluon plasma (QGP). Many of these models depend upon the corresponding running coupling constant.
Therefore the nature of coupling constant and associated renormalization group equations were always exciting topics in quantum field theory.

Using the renormalization group equation,
Collins and Perry 1975
published a significant study on ultra-dense nuclear matter (the idea of quark soup at high density) in 1975. The concept of thermally dependent renormalization group equations(RGE)
Kapusta 1979
;
Matsumoto et al. 1984
considered mass and coupling constant as temperature functions.
In deriving coupling constant, changes in the vertex function results in variations in coupling constant
Fujimoto and Yamada 1988
;
Baier et al. 1990
. This means, the coupling constant has a strong dependence on the vertex function
Nakkagawa and Niégawa 1987
;
Nakkagawa et al. 1988
. Also, the dependence of coupling constant on the gauge was investigated in
Chaichian and Hayashi 1996
;
Sasaki 1997
.

Braaten and Pisarski 1990
introduced significant ideas like hard thermal loops and, using it, they presented resummation of QCD thermal perturbation theory. A perturbative analysis of QCD at the static limit was shown in
van Eijck et al. 1994a
. The authors used both momentum and temperature dependent RGEs and derived a coupling constant that depends on momentum scale and temperature. During the same decade, several renormalization concepts were introduced, including environment friendly renormalization
van Eijck et al. 1994b
;
van Eijck et al. 1995
and idea of using more than one renormalization group for running mass and temperature
Stephens 1998
;
Stephens et al. 2004
. Recent developments include the research on magnetic field effects on coupling constant and expression for thermomagnetic coupling constant at one loop order
Ayala et al. 2018
;
Ferrer et al. 2015
.

On the description of QGP
Nadkarni 1983
;
Schneider and Weise 2001
;
Meisinger et al. 2004
;
Bannur 2007a
;
Bannur 2007b
;
Bannur 2007c
, most of the models take readily available coupling constants
Deur et al. 2016
from quantum chromodynamics (QCD) and evaluate their results. The most famous coupling constants in QCD have inverse logarithmic
Schneider 2002
;
Schneider 2003
thermal dependency.
Steffens 2006
, investigates the non-logarithmic and logarithmic nature of the QCD coupling of the high-temperature limit.
We can find works related to quark dynamics in a magnetic field which apply magnetic field-dependent coupling constants
Ferrer et al. 2015
;
Ayala et al. 2018
in quasi-particle models
Koothottil and Bannur 2019
;
Koothottil and Bannur 2020
;
Kurian and Chandra 2017
;
Kurian et al. 2019
to determine the equation of state. These models consider quarks and gluons as quasi-particles with thermal mass depending on various parameters like coupling constant, temperature, and magnetic field.

In the context of non-thermal and thermal field theory, we pay special attention to the quartic(
\phi^{4}
) interaction (also known as toy model) theory for two loop order. In this work, we use the simple diagrammatic analysis
Kleinert and Schulte-Frohlinde 2001
in imaginary time formalism(ITF)
Kapusta and Gale 2006
to derive a coupling constant that obeys RGE for both thermal and non-thermal field theory under the same mass scale
\mu
and coupling constant
g
. We take advantage of the analytically consistent theory
Kaneko 1994
, evaluated at external momentum equal to zero. We can extend the same approach to QCD.

The self-energy part of quartic interaction tadpole can be found in papers
O’connor et al. 1993
;
Parwani 1992
;
Arnold and Zhai 1994
;
Peshier et al. 1996
;
Peshier et al. 1998
under various approximations like massive and massless Lagrangian density.
Frenkel et al. 1992
;
Arnold and Zhai 1994
calculates the free energy part of the
\phi^{4}
interactions, and
Braaten and Nieto 1995
contains the higher-order calculations. In our work, we define Lagrangian density as

\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{2}\left\{\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial^{\mu}\phi-m^{2}\phi^{2}\right\}-\frac{\lambda}{4!}\phi^{4}

(1)

and follow a systematic approach (
\lambda
is the non-renormalized coupling constant).
The Lagrangian density is massive itself and consists of a quadratic and quartic interaction term. Some of the Feynman diagram integral results and formulae derived and used in this work may be found in other publications
Kapusta and Gale 2006
;
Bugrij and Shadura 1995
;
Andersen et al. 2000
;
Andersen et al. 2001
;
Kleinert and Schulte-Frohlinde 2001
.

We employ the dimensional regularization method
Bollini and Giambiagi 1972
;
’t Hooft and Veltman 1972
;
’t Hooft 1973
, which successfully regularizes non-Abelian gauge theory while preserving symmetries. The minimal subtraction scheme (MS scheme) follows after regularization and in which it corresponds to canceling out the pole parts (
\epsilon
) through the method of counter terms
Bogoliubow and Parasiuk 1957
. Then we apply the corresponding RGE to finite proper vertex function (FPVF) in imaginary time formalism in which, the removal of divergences at
\epsilon\to 0
, the vertex function becomes finite
Collins 1974
;
Speer 1974
;
Breitenlohner and Maison 1977
;
Caswell and Kennedy 1982
.

In this work, we use subscripts QFT and ITF to represent Feynman diagrams expressed in
non-thermal QFT
and
imaginary time formalism
.
Abbreviations TLA, SMC, and FPVF, represent for the
two loop approximation
, the
same mass scale (
\mu
) and coupling constant (
g
)
and
finite proper vertex function
, respectively. Throughout this paper, we used Euclidean momentum
K=[\omega_{n_{k}},k]
to write
K^{2}=\omega_{n_{k}}^{2}+k^{2}
. One can see three
\beta
’s appearing in this work.
\beta
denotes
1/T
.
\beta(g)
denotes the beta function associated with RGE.
\beta_{2}
and
\beta_{3}
are the coefficients of
g^{2}
and
g^{3}
of beta function, respectively.

In the RGE, we have an equation connecting coupling constant with mass scale using
\beta(g)=\frac{dg}{d\ln\mu}
and equation connecting running mass with mass-scale
\gamma_{m}(g)=\frac{d\ln m}{d\ln\mu}
. In thermal and non-thermal field theory,
\beta(g)
and
\gamma_{m}(g)
have the same expression. Suppose we have an additional equation connecting the coupling constant with temperature. With the help of
\beta(g),\gamma_{m}(g)
, and this new temperature-dependent equation, it is possible to find the temperature-dependent running mass, mass scale, and coupling constant.

We introduce a new approach where we apply RGE simultaneously on thermal and non-thermal FPVF under SMC. It results in a polynomial expression in
g
of order four. After a close examination, one can find that coefficients of
g^{2},g,g^{0}
are zero. So the non-trivial solution reduces to a linear equation in
g
with coefficients that depends on running mass, temperature, and mass scale. A minor rearrangement brings out the relation between coupling, running mass, temperature, and mass scale.

We follow a systematic approach
Kleinert and Schulte-Frohlinde 2001
. To ease the calculation, we make relations between diagrams in thermal and non-thermal field theory. We try to express all the ITF diagrams as the sum of QFT diagrams with thermal coefficients.

In
Section

II
, we write down all essential diagrams, such as two and four-point functions in one and two loop order, in ITF and QFT. We express ITF diagrams as sums of QFT diagrams (with and without thermal coefficients). We derive the requisite counter term diagrams in
Section

III
to remove the divergences from the corresponding proper vertex function and derive renormalization constants in ITF. In
Section

IV
, we calculate renormalization constants through necessary diagrams.
Section

V
discusses the idea and outcome of applying RGE on the FPVF difference and how it will lead to the temperature-dependent coupling constant under SMC.
Section

VI
includes plots revealing the temperature relations, such as the coupling constant vs. temperature, running mass vs. temperature, mass-scale vs. temperature, and pressure vs. temperature. In
Section

VI
, we verify the qualitative behavior of running mass and coupling results. We substitute running mass on the self-consistent quasi-particle model
Bannur 2007b
;
Bannur 2007c
;
Bannur 2007a
pressure relation and check whether it will lead to the Stephen-Boltzmann limit at the higher temperature approximations.

Another essential nature is the flexibility of this model. It depends on the integration constants, and functions
\beta(g)
and
\gamma_{m}(g)
. Once we have data points, we can fit running mass, coupling, pressure results to the data points by choosing appropriate values for the integral constants.

II
Regularization

It is possible to write down all diagrams in the
\phi^{4}
theory by a set of fundamental non-trivial Feynman diagrams known as one-particle irreducible (1 PI) diagrams. In other words, they are the smallest building block of diagrams.

(3)

Eqs.

2
and
3
are two and four-point vertex functions, expressed using corresponding 1 PI diagrams.

In
\phi^{4}
ITF and non-thermal QFT, both these two and four-point functions and their corresponding integrals diverge. We follow the dimensional regularization procedure. In which we introduce a parameter to pinpoint the diverging term and separate the singularity
Bollini and Giambiagi 1972
;
’t Hooft and Veltman 1972
;
’t Hooft 1973
. In the following subsections
A
and
B
, we express the two and four-point vertex functions in ITF as the summation of corresponding non-thermal QFT vertex functions with thermal coefficients. In the following evaluations, the remaining
g\mu^{\epsilon}
will become
\lambda\approx g
at
\epsilon\to 0
. Similarly in case of finite terms,
\lambda^{2}\to(g\mu^{\epsilon})^{2}=g^{2}
as
\epsilon\to 0
.

II.1
One Loop Order Diagrams

II.1.1
Two-point function

We define the tadpole diagram in imaginary time formalism as

\hbox to28.85pt{\vbox to14.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 185.14293pt\lower-341.6331pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -236.22 -462.6 M -226.38 -462.6 C -226.38 -457.16 -230.78 -452.76 -236.22 -452.76 C -241.66 -452.76 -246.06 -457.16 -246.06 -462.6 C -246.06 -468.03 -241.66 -472.44 -236.22 -472.44 C -230.78 -472.44 -226.38 -468.03 -226.38 -462.6 Z M -236.22 -462.6}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -255.91 -472.44 L -216.54 -472.44}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(4)

and that of non-thermal
\phi^{4}
theory as

\displaystyle\hbox to28.85pt{\vbox to14.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 185.14293pt\lower-341.6331pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -236.22 -462.6 M -226.38 -462.6 C -226.38 -457.16 -230.78 -452.76 -236.22 -452.76 C -241.66 -452.76 -246.06 -457.16 -246.06 -462.6 C -246.06 -468.03 -241.66 -472.44 -236.22 -472.44 C -230.78 -472.44 -226.38 -468.03 -226.38 -462.6 Z M -236.22 -462.6}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -255.91 -472.44 L -216.54 -472.44}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(5)

we use the result from Appendix
A-1
and express Tadpole in ITF in terms of Tadpole in non-thermal QFT as

\displaystyle\hbox to28.85pt{\vbox to14.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 185.14293pt\lower-341.6331pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -236.22 -462.6 M -226.38 -462.6 C -226.38 -457.16 -230.78 -452.76 -236.22 -452.76 C -241.66 -452.76 -246.06 -457.16 -246.06 -462.6 C -246.06 -468.03 -241.66 -472.44 -236.22 -472.44 C -230.78 -472.44 -226.38 -468.03 -226.38 -462.6 Z M -236.22 -462.6}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(6)

where

\displaystyle S_{1}(m,T)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{n_{B}(\beta\epsilon_{p})}{\epsilon_{p}}\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}

(7)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{m}{2\pi n\beta}\right)K_{1}(n\beta m)

We can describe the divergence as a singularity of parameter
\epsilon
by substituting
\lambda=g\mu^{\epsilon}
at dimension
N
(say) to
N-\epsilon
. We get the regularization result

\displaystyle\hbox to28.85pt{\vbox to14.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 185.14293pt\lower-341.6331pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -236.22 -462.6 M -226.38 -462.6 C -226.38 -457.16 -230.78 -452.76 -236.22 -452.76 C -241.66 -452.76 -246.06 -457.16 -246.06 -462.6 C -246.06 -468.03 -241.66 -472.44 -236.22 -472.44 C -230.78 -472.44 -226.38 -468.03 -226.38 -462.6 Z M -236.22 -462.6}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -255.91 -472.44 L -216.54 -472.44}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{ITF}

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{2}g}{(4\pi)^{2}}\left[\frac{2}{\epsilon}+\psi(2)+\ln\left(\frac{4\pi\mu^{2}}{m^{2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle-g\mu^{\epsilon}S_{1}(m,T)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)

(8)

In which
\psi(n)
is Euler Digamma function,
g
the dimensionless coupling constant, and
n_{B}(x)=(e^{x}-1)^{-1}
. As
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, the first term diverges.

with

) as shown in Appendix

as

(11)

with

\displaystyle W(r,n_{r})=\int\frac{2n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{p})\left(r^{2}+2pr\cos\theta+\omega_{n_{r}}^{2}\right)}{\varepsilon_{p}(\left(r^{2}+2pr\cos\theta+\omega_{n_{r}}^{2})^{2}+4\varepsilon_{p}^{2}\omega_{n_{r}}^{2}\right)}\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}

(12)

Using dimensional regularization (
\lambda=g\mu^{\epsilon}
) and standard textbook results
Kleinert and Schulte-Frohlinde 2001
, we can write

\displaystyle\hbox to24.87pt{\vbox to14.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 183.15102pt\lower-341.6331pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -236.22 -462.6 M -226.38 -462.6 C -226.38 -457.16 -230.78 -452.76 -236.22 -452.76 C -241.66 -452.76 -246.06 -457.16 -246.06 -462.6 C -246.06 -468.03 -241.66 -472.44 -236.22 -472.44 C -230.78 -472.44 -226.38 -468.03 -226.38 -462.6 Z M -236.22 -462.6}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -246.06 -462.6 L -253.15 -456.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -246.06 -462.6 L -253.15 -469.29}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -226.38 -462.6 L -219.29 -456.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -226.38 -462.6 L -219.29 -469.29}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{QFT}

\displaystyle=\frac{g^{2}\mu^{\epsilon}}{(4\pi)^{2}}\left(\frac{2}{\epsilon}+\psi(1)+\int_{0}^{1}dx\ln\left[\frac{4\pi\mu^{2}}{Q^{2}x(1-x)+m^{2}}\right]\right)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)

(13)

with
Q=[\omega_{n_{q}},\vec{q}]
.

The external momentum
\vec{k}
is the sum of the incoming momenta
\vec{k}_{1}+\vec{k}_{2}
and the Matsubara summation parameter
\omega_{n_{k}}=\omega_{n_{k_{1}}}+\omega_{n_{k_{2}}}
, where
\omega_{n_{k}}=2\pi n_{k}T
with
n_{k}
being an integer. For Matsubara summation, we use the symbol
\sumint_{p}=\sum_{n_{p}=-\infty}^{\infty}\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}
.

II.2
Two Loop Order Diagrams

II.2.1
Two point function

In TLA, we have to evaluate the following diagrams and express them in terms of thermal independent QFT diagrams with temperature function coefficients. The integral expression of
in ITF and QFT are

\displaystyle\sumint_{p_{1},p_{2}}\frac{\lambda^{2}T^{2}}{\varepsilon_{p1}^{2}+\omega_{n_{p_{1}}}^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{p2}^{2}+\omega_{n_{p_{2}}}^{2}}\right]^{2}

(14)

and

\displaystyle\int\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\varepsilon_{p1}^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{p2}^{2}}\right]^{2}\frac{d^{4}p_{1}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{d^{4}p_{2}}{(2\pi)^{4}}

(15)

respectively with
\varepsilon_{p}^{2}=p^{2}+m^{2}
. We follow the same convention as shown in
Section

II.1
. As specified in Appendices
A-3
and
A-4
, after regularization (
\lambda\to g\mu^{\epsilon},\ \frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\to\frac{d^{3-\epsilon}p}{(2\pi)^{3-\epsilon}},\ \frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\to\frac{d^{4-\epsilon}p}{(2\pi)^{4-\epsilon}},\epsilon\to 0
) we get the results

\displaystyle\hbox to28.85pt{\vbox to28.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 185.14293pt\lower-341.6331pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -236.22 -462.6 M -226.38 -462.6 C -226.38 -457.16 -230.78 -452.76 -236.22 -452.76 C -241.66 -452.76 -246.06 -457.16 -246.06 -462.6 C -246.06 -468.03 -241.66 -472.44 -236.22 -472.44 C -230.78 -472.44 -226.38 -468.03 -226.38 -462.6 Z M -236.22 -462.6}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}

\displaystyle+(g\mu^{\epsilon})^{2}\frac{S_{1}(m,T)S_{0}(m,T)}{4\pi}

The expression for
in ITF and QFT are

\displaystyle\sumint_{p_{1},p_{2}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{p_{1}}^{2}+\omega_{n_{p_{1}}}^{2}}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\varepsilon_{p_{2}}^{2}+\omega_{n_{p_{2}}}^{2}}\frac{T^{2}}{\varepsilon_{p_{1}+p_{2}+q}^{2}+\omega_{n_{p_{1}+p_{2}+q}}^{2}}

(17)

and

\displaystyle\int\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\varepsilon_{p_{1}}^{2}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{p_{2}}^{2}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{p_{1}+p_{2}+q}^{2}}\frac{d^{4}p_{1}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{d^{4}p_{2}}{(2\pi)^{4}}

(18)

respectively.
From the result of QFT
Kleinert and Schulte-Frohlinde 2001
;
Andersen et al. 2001
;
Andersen et al. 2000
and corresponding ITF, after regularization (
\lambda\to g\mu^{\epsilon}
,
d^{3}p\to d^{3-\epsilon}p
,
d^{4}p\to d^{4-\epsilon}p
), we express the sunset/sunrise diagram at external momentum zero as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{6}\hbox to28.85pt{\vbox to14.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 185.14293pt\lower-348.74629pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -236.22 -472.44 M -226.38 -472.44 C -226.38 -467.01 -230.78 -462.6 -236.22 -462.6 C -241.66 -462.6 -246.06 -467.01 -246.06 -472.44 C -246.06 -477.88 -241.66 -482.28 -236.22 -482.28 C -230.78 -482.28 -226.38 -477.88 -226.38 -472.44 Z M -236.22 -472.44}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}

(19)

\displaystyle+\frac{S_{1}(m,T)}{2}\frac{g^{2}\mu^{\epsilon}}{(4\pi)^{2}}\left[\psi(1)+\ln\left(\frac{4\pi\mu^{2}}{m^{2}}\right)+\frac{2}{\epsilon}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{S_{1}(m,T)}{2}\frac{g^{2}\mu^{\epsilon}}{(4\pi)^{2}}\left(2-\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{3}}\right)+\frac{g^{2}m^{2}}{64\pi^{4}}Y(m,T)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)

where

\displaystyle Y(m,T)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}U(x)U(y)G(x,y)\ dx\ dy

(20)

\displaystyle U(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sinh(x)}{\exp\left(\beta m\cosh(x)\right)-1}

(21)

\displaystyle G(x,y)

\displaystyle=\ln\left(\frac{1+2\cosh(x-y)}{1+2\cosh(x+y)}\frac{1-2\cosh(x+y)}{1-2\cosh(x-y)}\right)

(22)

\displaystyle S_{N}(m,T)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{m}{2\pi j\beta}\right)^{N}K_{N}(j\beta m)

(23)

In these two cases, the remaining
g\mu^{\epsilon}
will become
\lambda\approx g
at
\epsilon\to 0
. Similarly in case of finite terms,
(g\mu^{\epsilon})^{2}=\lambda^{2}=g^{2}
as
\epsilon\to 0
.
Detailed expansion and results are given in Appendices
A-3
and
A-4
.

III
Counter terms and minimal subtraction scheme

Counter term diagrams are those that make the vertex function finite when added with the vertex function.
The previously derived diagrams in
Section

II
contain terms that diverge for
\epsilon\to 0
.
We redefine the proper vertex function to divergence removed proper vertex function(FPVF). We use the MS-scheme to deal with diverging terms. Here, operator
\mathcal{K}
separates the diverging terms.

i.e.;

\widetilde{\Gamma}^{(n)}=\Gamma^{(n)}-\mathcal{K}\left(\Gamma^{(n)}\right).

(33)

By this definition

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Gamma}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\Gamma^{(2)}-\mathcal{K}\left(\Gamma^{(2)}\right)

(34)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Gamma}^{(4)}

\displaystyle=\Gamma^{(4)}-\mathcal{K}\left(\Gamma^{(4)}\right)

(35)

III.1
One loop calculation

The counter terms in one loop order are usually the pole term in diagrams with a negative sign.

III.2
Two loop calculation

The redefinition of proper vertex function in one loop order (
Eqs.

36
and
40
) to make it finite causes more counter-term diagrams to emerge in the two loop order calculation.

The pole term (
1/\epsilon^{n}
,
n>0
) can have both thermal and non-thermal coefficients. Substituting
-m^{2}c_{m^{2}}^{1}
of
Eq.

37
for
-g\mu^{\epsilon}
in
Eq.

9
scattering diagram at
n_{k}\text{,}k=0
gives one of the counter diagrams required. Appendix
B-2
contains the detailed derivation.

Eq.

48
.
c_{m^{2}}
and
c_{\phi}
absorb the remaining divergences. So the total contribution to counter terms
c_{m^{2}}
and
c_{\phi}
up to
g^{2}
in ITF is identical to that of QFT at
k_{0}=\omega_{n_{k}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})_{QFT,K=[\omega_{n_{k}},\vec{k}]}\end{split}

(49)

=-\left[\frac{g}{(4\pi)^{2}}\frac{m^{2}}{\epsilon}+\frac{g^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}\left(\frac{2m^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{m^{2}}{2\epsilon}-\frac{K^{2}}{12\epsilon}\right)\right]

with
K^{2}=\omega_{n_{k}}^{2}+\vec{k}^{2}
.

i.e., in ITF, if we follow the textbook procedure (
Kleinert and Schulte-Frohlinde 2001
), the sum of counter terms are identical to that of QFT, with
k_{0}=\omega_{n_{k}}
. If we extract the polynomial with coefficient
m^{2}
and
K^{2}
, we get (
Kleinert and Schulte-Frohlinde 2001
)

\displaystyle m^{2}(c_{m^{2}}^{1}+c_{m^{2}}^{2})=m^{2}\left[\frac{g}{(4\pi)^{2}}\frac{1}{\epsilon}+\frac{g^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}\left(\frac{2}{\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\right)\right]

(50)

For field renormalization, we have to consider the term proportional to
K^{2}
, so

\displaystyle K^{2}c_{\phi}^{2}=\frac{1}{6}\mathcal{K}\left(\hbox to28.85pt{\vbox to14.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 185.14293pt\lower-348.74629pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -236.22 -472.44 M -226.38 -472.44 C -226.38 -467.01 -230.78 -462.6 -236.22 -462.6 C -241.66 -462.6 -246.06 -467.01 -246.06 -472.44 C -246.06 -477.88 -241.66 -482.28 -236.22 -482.28 C -230.78 -482.28 -226.38 -477.88 -226.38 -472.44 Z M -236.22 -472.44}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -255.91 -472.44 L -216.54 -472.44}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{QFT}\right)|_{m=0,k_{0}=\omega_{n_{k}}}=-\frac{g^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}\frac{K^{2}}{12\epsilon}

(51)

III.2.2
Four-point function

To proceed further, we have to remove the divergences of the four-point function using the renormalization procedure
Kleinert and Schulte-Frohlinde 2001
. The result is the same as that of non-thermal QFT. i.e.,

\displaystyle Z_{m^{2}}(g,\epsilon^{-1})

\displaystyle=1+\frac{g}{(4\pi)^{2}}\frac{1}{\epsilon}+\frac{g^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}\left(\frac{2}{\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\right)

(63)

From
Sections

III.1.1
and
51
, we get

\displaystyle Z_{\phi}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{1}{K^{2}}\frac{1}{6}\mathcal{K}\left(\hbox to28.85pt{\vbox to14.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 185.14293pt\lower-348.74629pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -236.22 -472.44 M -226.38 -472.44 C -226.38 -467.01 -230.78 -462.6 -236.22 -462.6 C -241.66 -462.6 -246.06 -467.01 -246.06 -472.44 C -246.06 -477.88 -241.66 -482.28 -236.22 -482.28 C -230.78 -482.28 -226.38 -477.88 -226.38 -472.44 Z M -236.22 -472.44}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(64)

\displaystyle=1+c_{\phi}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{g^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{12\epsilon}

The above results show that for two, and four-point functions at TLA, the values of
Z_{\phi},\ Z_{g},\ \text{and }Z_{m^{2}}
for ITF are the same as for non-thermal
\phi^{4}
theory
Kleinert and Schulte-Frohlinde 2001
. So we demand that the renormalization group equation(RGE) for QFT must also be true for ITF.

V
Renormalization Group Equation in Two loop order two-point function

The previous results show that neither
Z_{\phi}
,
Z_{g}
, nor
Z_{m^{2}}
are explicitly temperature-dependent, and they agree with the non-thermal field theory. However, a non-explicit reliance may exist via the coupling constant
g
as
g(\mu(T))
. Furthermore, we use RGE for TLA to see if such relationships are possible. i.e., we demand
Eqs.

66
,
67
,
68
,
69
and
70
to be true for TLA under SMC. Here the RGE is explicitly temperature independent, but the vertex function in ITF is temperature-dependent.

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d(\ln\mu)}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{(n)}\left(m,g,T,\mu\right)=

\displaystyle\left[\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}+\beta(g)\frac{\partial}{\partial g}-n\gamma(g)+\gamma_{m}m\frac{\partial}{\partial m}\right]\widetilde{\Gamma}^{(n)}\left(m,g,T,\mu\right)\approx_{TLA}0

(65)

\displaystyle\left[\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}+\beta(g)\frac{\partial}{\partial g}-2\gamma(g)+\gamma_{m}m\frac{\partial}{\partial m}\right]\widetilde{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{ITF}\left(m,g,T,\mu\right)\approx_{TLA}0

(66)

\displaystyle\left[\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}+\beta(g)\frac{\partial}{\partial g}-2\gamma(g)+\gamma_{m}m\frac{\partial}{\partial m}\right]\widetilde{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{QFT}\left(m,g,T,\mu\right)\approx_{TLA}0

(67)

\displaystyle\frac{dg}{d\ln(\mu)}=\beta(g)\approx\beta_{2}g^{2}+\beta_{3}g^{3}

(68)

\displaystyle\frac{d\ln(m(\mu))}{d\ln(\mu)}=\gamma_{m}(g)\approx\gamma_{m_{1}}g+\gamma_{m_{2}}g^{2}

(69)

\displaystyle\gamma(g)\approx\gamma_{2}g^{2}

(70)

In the previous section, we expressed ITF diagrams as the sum of corresponding QFT diagrams with subdiagrams having thermal coefficients. Therefore we can write the finite proper vertex function (FPVF) in ITF as the sum of FPVF of QFT with some subdiagrams having thermal coefficients under SMC.

At external momentum zero, we can write

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Gamma}^{(2)ITF}_{finite,K=0}=\widetilde{\Gamma}^{(2)QFT}_{finite,K=0}+\Gamma^{\text{diff}}

(71)

where
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{finite}=\Gamma^{(2)}-\mathcal{K}\left(\Gamma^{(2)}\right)
.

Thus any RGE which is valid for both ITF and QFT will also be true for their differences
\left(\widetilde{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{ITF}-\widetilde{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{QFT}\right)
.
Then
Eqs.

66
and
67
leads to

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{\partial}{\partial ln(\mu)}+\beta(g)\frac{\partial}{\partial g}-2\gamma(g)+\gamma_{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial ln(m)}\right\}\Gamma^{\text{diff}}=0

(72)

From the previous section, it is clear that the individual Feynman diagram evaluated in ITF and QFT at
k_{0}=\omega_{n_{k}}
is different. Still, the two-point and four-point vertex divergences are of the same form. Thus the renormalization constants (
Z_{m^{2}},Z_{g},...c_{m^{2}},c_{g}
) are in the same structure. Therefore we can use the results of non-thermal
\phi^{4}
theory
for finite functions
\beta(g),\ \gamma(g),\text{and}\ \gamma_{m}(g)
from
Kleinert and Schulte-Frohlinde 2001
for ITF also.

At
K\neq 0
, the functions are

\displaystyle\gamma(g)

\displaystyle=\frac{g^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{12}=\gamma_{2}g^{2}

(73)

\displaystyle\gamma_{m}(g)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{g}{(4\pi)^{2}}-\frac{5}{12}\frac{g^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}=g\gamma_{m1}+g^{2}\gamma_{m2}

\displaystyle\beta(g)

\displaystyle=-\epsilon g+\frac{3g^{2}}{(4\pi)^{2}}-\frac{17g^{3}}{3(4\pi)^{4}}=g\beta_{1}+g^{2}\beta_{2}+g^{3}\beta_{3}

When
K\to 0
,
\epsilon\to 0
, as shown in
Appendix

C
, the functions change to

\displaystyle\gamma(g)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{2}g^{2}=0

(74)

\displaystyle\gamma_{m}(g)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{g}{(4\pi)^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{g^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}=g\gamma_{m1}+g^{2}\gamma_{m2}

\displaystyle\beta(g)

\displaystyle=\frac{3g^{2}}{(4\pi)^{2}}-\frac{6g^{3}}{(4\pi)^{4}}=g^{2}\beta_{2}+g^{3}\beta_{3}

The general form of the two-point FPVF at TLA is

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Gamma}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=(\hbox to28.85pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 185.14293pt\lower-341.6331pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -255.91 -472.44 L -216.54 -472.44}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})^{-1}-\frac{1}{2}\left[\hbox to28.85pt{\vbox to14.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 185.14293pt\lower-341.6331pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -236.22 -462.6 M -226.38 -462.6 C -226.38 -457.16 -230.78 -452.76 -236.22 -452.76 C -241.66 -452.76 -246.06 -457.16 -246.06 -462.6 C -246.06 -468.03 -241.66 -472.44 -236.22 -472.44 C -230.78 -472.44 -226.38 -468.03 -226.38 -462.6 Z M -236.22 -462.6}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -255.91 -472.44 L -216.54 -472.44}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

In order to simplify these equations, let us define an operator

\displaystyle\Delta(\mathbb{A})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{A}_{ITF}|_{k,\omega_{n_{k}}=0}-\mathcal{K}\left(\mathbb{A}_{ITF}\right)|_{k,\omega_{n_{k}}=0}

(76)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{A}_{QFT}|_{k_{0},k=0}+\mathcal{K}\left(\mathbb{A}_{QFT}\right)|_{k_{0},k=0}

where
\mathbb{A}
represents the appropriate diagram.

Since we defined
\Gamma^{\text{diff}}_{n_{k},\vec{k}=0}=\Gamma^{(2)ITF}|_{n_{k},k=0}-\Gamma^{(2)QFT}|_{k_{0},k=0}
we get

\displaystyle-\Gamma^{\text{diff}}_{n_{k},\vec{k}=0}=\frac{\Delta}{2}\left(\hbox to28.85pt{\vbox to14.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 185.14293pt\lower-341.6331pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -236.22 -462.6 M -226.38 -462.6 C -226.38 -457.16 -230.78 -452.76 -236.22 -452.76 C -241.66 -452.76 -246.06 -457.16 -246.06 -462.6 C -246.06 -468.03 -241.66 -472.44 -236.22 -472.44 C -230.78 -472.44 -226.38 -468.03 -226.38 -462.6 Z M -236.22 -462.6}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(77)

From Appendix
A-1

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Delta\left(\hbox to28.85pt{\vbox to14.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 185.14293pt\lower-341.6331pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -236.22 -462.6 M -226.38 -462.6 C -226.38 -457.16 -230.78 -452.76 -236.22 -452.76 C -241.66 -452.76 -246.06 -457.16 -246.06 -462.6 C -246.06 -468.03 -241.66 -472.44 -236.22 -472.44 C -230.78 -472.44 -226.38 -468.03 -226.38 -462.6 Z M -236.22 -462.6}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -255.91 -472.44 L -216.54 -472.44}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(78)

From Appendix
A-3
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\Delta\left(\hbox to28.85pt{\vbox to28.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 185.14293pt\lower-341.6331pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -236.22 -462.6 M -226.38 -462.6 C -226.38 -457.16 -230.78 -452.76 -236.22 -452.76 C -241.66 -452.76 -246.06 -457.16 -246.06 -462.6 C -246.06 -468.03 -241.66 -472.44 -236.22 -472.44 C -230.78 -472.44 -226.38 -468.03 -226.38 -462.6 Z M -236.22 -462.6}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -255.91 -472.44 L -216.54 -472.44}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -236.22 -442.91 M -226.38 -442.91 C -226.38 -437.48 -230.78 -433.07 -236.22 -433.07 C -241.66 -433.07 -246.06 -437.48 -246.06 -442.91 C -246.06 -448.35 -241.66 -452.76 -236.22 -452.76 C -230.78 -452.76 -226.38 -448.35 -226.38 -442.91 Z M -236.22 -442.91}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{g^{2}}{16\pi}S_{0}(m,T)S_{1}(m,T)

(79)

\displaystyle-\frac{g^{2}m^{2}S_{0}(m,T)}{4(4\pi)^{3}}\left[\psi(2)+\ln\left(\frac{4\pi\mu^{2}}{m^{2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{g^{2}S_{1}(m,T)}{4(4\pi)^{2}}\left[\psi(1)+\ln\left(\frac{4\pi\mu^{2}}{m^{2}}\right)\right]

From Appendix
A-4
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta}{6}\left(\hbox to28.85pt{\vbox to14.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 185.14293pt\lower-348.74629pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -236.22 -472.44 M -226.38 -472.44 C -226.38 -467.01 -230.78 -462.6 -236.22 -462.6 C -241.66 -462.6 -246.06 -467.01 -246.06 -472.44 C -246.06 -477.88 -241.66 -482.28 -236.22 -482.28 C -230.78 -482.28 -226.38 -477.88 -226.38 -472.44 Z M -236.22 -472.44}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -255.91 -472.44 L -216.54 -472.44}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{g^{2}S_{1}(m,T)}{2(4\pi)^{2}}\left(\psi(1)+\ln\left(\frac{4\pi\mu^{2}}{m^{2}}\right)\right)

(80)

\displaystyle+\frac{g^{2}S_{1}(m,T)}{2(4\pi)^{2}}\left(2-\frac{\sqrt{3}\pi}{3}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{g^{2}m^{2}}{64\pi^{4}}Y(m,T)

with

\displaystyle Y(m,T)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}U(x)U(y)G(x,y)\ dx\ dy

(81)

\displaystyle U(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sinh(x)}{\exp\left(\beta m\cosh(x)\right)-1}

(82)

\displaystyle G(x,y)

\displaystyle=\ln\left(\frac{1+2\cosh(x-y)}{1+2\cosh(x+y)}\frac{1-2\cosh(x+y)}{1-2\cosh(x-y)}\right)

(83)

Therefore by combining the above results, we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Gamma^{\text{diff}}_{n_{k},\vec{k}=0}&=\ \frac{g}{2}\ S_{1}(m,T)\\
&-\frac{3g^{2}}{4}\frac{S_{1}(m,T)}{(4\pi)^{2}}\left[\psi(1)+\ln\left(\frac{4\pi\mu^{2}}{m^{2}}\right)\right]\\
&-\frac{g^{2}}{4(4\pi)}S_{0}(m,T)S_{1}(m,T)\\
&+\frac{g^{2}m^{2}}{4(4\pi)^{3}}S_{0}(m,T)\left[\psi(2)+\ln\left(\frac{4\pi\mu^{2}}{m^{2}}\right)\right]\\
&-\frac{g^{2}m^{2}}{64\pi^{4}}Y(m,T)\\
&-\frac{g^{2}}{32\pi^{2}}S_{1}(m,T)\left[2-\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{3}}\right]\end{split}

(84)

Thus combining
Eq.

84
and
Eq.

72
and expanding it as a polynomial in
g
looks like a polynomial of order four. However, as shown in
Appendix

D
, the coefficients of
g^{2},g
and
g^{0}
are zero and lead to first-degree polynomial. When we rearrange the linear equation in
g
, we get

g(m,T,\mu)=\frac{A(m,T)\ln(\mu)+B(m,T)}{C(m,T)\ln(\mu)+D(m,T)}

(85)

with

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\left[-\gamma_{m1}V_{2,\ln m}-2\beta_{2}V_{2}(m,T)\right]

(86)

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=\left[2(\beta_{3}-\gamma_{2})V_{2}+\gamma_{m2}V_{2,\ln m}\right]

(87)

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=(2\gamma_{2}-\beta_{3})T_{1}-2\beta_{2}V_{1}(m,T)

(88)

\displaystyle-\gamma_{m1}V_{1,\ln m}-\gamma_{m2}T_{1,\ln m}

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=2(\beta_{3}-\gamma_{2})V_{1}+\gamma_{m2}V_{1,\ln m}

(89)

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}S_{1}(m,T)

(90)

\displaystyle T_{1,\ln m}

\displaystyle=-\frac{m^{2}}{4\pi}S_{0}(m,T)

(91)

\displaystyle\begin{split}V_{1}(m,T)&=\frac{m^{2}}{4(4\pi)^{3}}S_{0}(m,T)\left[\psi(2)+\ln\left(\frac{4\pi}{m^{2}}\right)\right]\\
&-\frac{3}{4}\frac{S_{1}(m,T)}{(4\pi)^{2}}\left[\psi(1)+\ln\left(\frac{4\pi}{m^{2}}\right)\right]\\
&-\frac{1}{4(4\pi)}S_{0}(m,T)S_{1}(m,T)-\frac{m^{2}}{64\pi^{4}}Y(m,T)\\
&-\frac{S_{1}(m,T)}{32\pi^{2}}\left[2-\frac{\sqrt{3}\pi}{3}\right]\\
\end{split}

(92)

V_{2}(m,T)=\left(\frac{m^{2}}{2(4\pi)^{3}}S_{0}(m,T)-\frac{3S_{1}(m,T)}{2(4\pi)^{2}}\right)

(93)

V_{2,\ln m}=\frac{4m^{2}S_{0}(m,T)}{(4\pi)^{3}}-\frac{m^{4}S_{-1}(m,T)}{(4\pi)^{4}}

(94)

\displaystyle V_{1,\ln m}

\displaystyle=\frac{2m^{2}}{(4\pi)^{3}}S_{0}(m,T)\left[\psi(1)+\ln\left(\frac{4\pi}{m^{2}}\right)\right]

(95)

\displaystyle-\frac{m^{4}}{2(4\pi)^{4}}S_{-1}(m,T)\left[\psi(2)+\ln\left(\frac{4\pi}{m^{2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{2}\frac{S_{1}(m,T)}{(4\pi)^{2}}+\frac{m^{2}S_{0}^{2}(m,T)}{2(4\pi)^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{m^{2}}{2(4\pi)^{2}}S_{1}(m,T)S_{-1}(m,T)

\displaystyle+\frac{m^{2}S_{0}(m,T)}{(4\pi)^{3}}\left[2-\frac{\sqrt{3}\pi}{3}\right]-\frac{m^{2}}{32\pi^{4}}Y(m,T)

\displaystyle-\frac{m^{4}}{32\pi^{4}}\frac{\partial Y(m,T)}{\partial m^{2}}

The other trivial value of
g
that satisfies these equations is zero, so we do not care about those solutions. Instead, choosing another physically possible solution (as in
Eq.

85
) leads to a thermal-dependent coupling constant.
If we combine with beta coupling constant relation such as

\frac{d\ g(\mu)}{d\ \ln(\mu)}=\beta_{2}g^{2}+\beta_{3}g^{3}

(96)

give rise to the result

\displaystyle\ln(\mu)

\displaystyle=\int^{g}\frac{1}{\beta_{2}t^{2}+\beta_{3}t^{3}}dt

(97)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\beta_{2}\ g}+\frac{\beta_{3}}{\beta_{2}^{2}}\ln\left(\beta_{3}+\frac{\beta_{2}}{g}\right)+\ln\mu_{0}

Similarly, the corresponding running mass coupling relation is

\frac{d\ \ln(m)}{d\ \ln(\mu)}=\gamma_{m}(g)

(98)

When combined with
Eq.

96
, it results in

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\ \ln(m)}{\partial g}\frac{dg}{d\ \ln(\mu)}=\gamma_{m}(g)

(99)

\displaystyle\implies\frac{\partial\ \ln(m)}{\partial g}=\frac{\gamma_{m}(g)}{\beta(g)}=\frac{\gamma_{m1}+\gamma_{m2}g}{\beta_{2}g+\beta_{3}g^{2}}

\displaystyle\ln\left(\frac{m}{m_{0}}\right)=\chi_{2}+\frac{\gamma_{m1}}{\beta_{2}}\ln(g)+\left(\frac{\gamma_{m2}}{\beta_{3}}-\frac{\gamma_{m1}}{\beta_{2}}\right)\ln(\beta_{3}\ g+\beta_{2})

(100)

where
\ln\mu_{0}
,
m_{0}
, and
\chi_{2}
are the respective integration constants.

Solving
Eqs.

97
,
100
and
85
simultaneously, we get temperature-dependent running mass and coupling constant.

Combining the above results with the quasi-particle model of Bannur
Bannur 2007c
;
Bannur 2007a
;
Bannur 2007b
, we get expression for energy density and pressure as

\displaystyle\varepsilon(T)

\displaystyle=g_{f}\frac{m^{4}}{2\pi^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left[\frac{3K_{2}(\frac{nm}{T})}{(\frac{nm}{T})^{2}}+\frac{K_{1}(\frac{nm}{T})}{\frac{nm}{T}}\right]

(101)

\displaystyle=g_{f}\frac{m^{4}}{16\pi^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left[K_{4}\left(\frac{nm}{T}\right)-K_{0}\left(\frac{nm}{T}\right)\right]

(102)

and

\displaystyle\frac{P}{T}-\frac{P_{0}}{T_{0}}=\int_{T_{0}}^{T}\frac{\varepsilon(m(T),T)}{T^{2}}\ dT

(103)

VI
Results and Discussion

We have derived an equation relating running mass, mass scale, coupling, and temperature, i.e.,
Eq.

85
. Similarly, we have the equation connecting running coupling with mass-scale known as beta function equation as in
Eq.

97
. Furthermore, we have an equation connecting running mass and coupling as in
Eq.

100
. Solving
Eqs.

85
,
97
and
100
for each temperature value will give us the results as shown in the figures.
If one wishes to keep the
\mu
independent from this set of equations, then as pointed out in
Kleinert and Schulte-Frohlinde 2001
, one can redefine it as
\mu\to\sigma\mu
, in that case, our above expressions will be a particular case of
\sigma=1
. One will have to rewrite all equations of RGE concerning these changes, and a new differential equation between
\sigma
and
\mu
will appear, as shown in
Kleinert and Schulte-Frohlinde 2001
.

Fig. 1:
Two loop coupling constant results.
g
against
T/m_{0}
plotted with varying values of integration constants
\ln\mu_{0}
and
\chi_{2}
with
m_{0}\approx 1

Fig. 2:
Two loop running mass results. The difference between the curves is due to the different integration constants, as shown in the figure.

Fig. 3:
Pressure scaled by
\frac{\pi^{2}}{90}T^{4}
against
T/m_{0}
, with varying values of
T_{0},P_{0},\ln\mu_{0},\chi_{2}
.

Fig. 4:
\frac{\mu}{\mu_{0}}
plotted against
T/m_{0}
with varying integration constants
\ln(\mu_{0})
and
\chi_{2}
with
m_{0}\approx 1

We plot the main results in Fig.1-4, with varying integration constants. Two loop coupling constant results and two loop running mass results are shown in Fig. 1 and Fig. 2, respectively. It is evident from Fig. 2 that as temperature goes to infinity, the running mass per temperature goes to zero. To understand the nature of pressure proposed by the model, we plot pressure against temperature with different values of
T_{0}
,
P_{0}
in Fig. 3. These results show similar trends, i.e.,
\beta\to 0
, i.e.,
T\to\infty
pressure reaches the ideal limit
P_{SB}=P_{ideal}=\frac{\pi^{2}}{90}T^{4}
, irrespective of the initial value.
P_{SB}
is Stefan Boltzmann’s limit of pressure. In Fig. 4, with a given
\ln\mu_{0}
and
\chi_{2}
, at high temperatures, the ratio
\mu/\mu_{0}
goes to a constant value. The same trend can be seen in Fig. 1, i.e., going to a constant value at a high-temperature limit. Fitting with lattice data can be used to find appropriate integration constants
\mu_{0}
and
\chi_{2}
.
When
T\to 0
, as per
Appendix D-1
,
g\approx-(\gamma_{m1}/\gamma_{m2})
. As a result, at T=0,
\ln(\mu/\mu_{0})
becomes a complex number. However, one can make the
\mu
a real number by choosing
\mu_{0}
, a negative number/complex number/complex function that can cancel the complex factor appearing on the right-hand side
Eq.

97
.
The same holds for running mass. So by choosing the appropriate integration constants/functions at zero temperature limit, one can still make the mass scale and running mass real or complex. These integral constants used in our model make it flexible. However, the best way to determine the value of integration constants will be to fit the actual data with the corresponding function once it becomes available. In our model rather than selecting the mass scale ourselves, we set the mass scale
\mu
to be a parameter that obeys RGE. The temperature dependence came naturally.

The quasi-particle model
Bannur 2007c
;
Bannur 2007a
;
Bannur 2007b
derives energy density from standard statistical mechanics at relativistic Bose-Einstein distribution. The pressure
Eq.

103
has the property that at
T\to T_{0}
, the integral part of the equation becomes zero. Hence, the pressure converges to the
P_{0}
value. From
Appendix D-2
, it is clear that as
T\to 0
, energy density
\varepsilon(T)\to 0
and
\frac{\varepsilon(T)}{T^{2}}\to 0
.

VII
Conclusion

We conclude that the renormalization constants in imaginary time formalism with one and two loop approximations are identical to those in non-thermal QFT. In our new approach, by applying RGE on thermal and non-thermal vertex functions simultaneously, we derived the coupling constant and corresponding running mass at zero momentum limits, where self-energy is analytic
Kaneko 1994
. Running mass and coupling constant with temperature dependence with varying integration constants are plotted in figures for massive
\phi^{4}
field theory. Combining these results with the quasi-particle model of Bannur
Bannur 2007b
;
Bannur 2007c
;
Bannur 2007a
(
Eqs.

101
and
103
) and by choosing different values to
P_{0}
and
T_{0}
, we have evaluated the pressure for the temperature dependent running mass. At higher temperatures, we observe pressure reaching its ideal limit
\frac{\pi^{2}}{90}T^{4}
irrespective of its initial value of
P_{0}/T_{0}
. Our model is flexible such a way that once the experimental data or lattice data are available for some range, we will be able to tweak the integration constants in our model and compare. It will be interesting to know the results when we extend this model to QCD in the future.

Integral equations ITF to Non thermal QFT Connections

Here we derive the necessary diagrams for the calculation. Also, we used approximations
\lambda=g\mu^{\epsilon}
on regularization, especially at the final stage. We used an operator
\mathcal{K}
, which picks up the diverging terms from the corresponding graphs it applied. i.e.,
\mathcal{K}\left(\frac{A}{\epsilon^{n}}+B+c\epsilon\right)=\frac{A}{\epsilon^{n}}
.

with
R=[\omega_{n_{r}},r]

(A.122)

\displaystyle I_{3}=\lambda^{2}\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{d^{3}q}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{3n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{p})n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{q})}{4\varepsilon_{p}\varepsilon_{q}}\times\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}=\pm 1}\frac{1}{(i\sigma_{1}\varepsilon_{p}+i\sigma_{2}\varepsilon_{q}+\omega_{n_{s}})^{2}+(\vec{p}+\vec{q}+\vec{s})^{2}+m^{2}}

with

\displaystyle P=[i\sigma_{1}\varepsilon_{p},\vec{p}]\ Q=[i\sigma_{2}\varepsilon_{q},\vec{q}],\ S=[\omega_{n_{s}},\vec{s}]

(A.123)

Kleinert and Schulte-Frohlinde 2001
and Appendix

Using
Kleinert and Schulte-Frohlinde 2001
and results from Appendices
A-1
and
A-2

where

.

\displaystyle\hbox to28.85pt{\vbox to17.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 185.14293pt\lower-348.74629pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -236.22 -472.44 M -226.38 -472.44 C -226.38 -467.01 -230.78 -462.6 -236.22 -462.6 C -241.66 -462.6 -246.06 -467.01 -246.06 -472.44 C -246.06 -477.88 -241.66 -482.28 -236.22 -482.28 C -230.78 -482.28 -226.38 -477.88 -226.38 -472.44 Z M -236.22 -472.44}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(A.141)

where

\displaystyle I_{11}=\int\frac{1}{P^{2}+m^{2}}\frac{1}{Q^{2}+m^{2}}\frac{1}{R^{2}+m^{2}}\frac{1}{S^{2}+m^{2}}\frac{d^{4}P}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{d^{4}Q}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{d^{4}R}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{d^{4}S}{(2\pi)^{4}}

(A.142)

\displaystyle\times(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{4}(R+P-K_{1}-K_{2})(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{4}(S+Q-P-K_{3})

with

\displaystyle R=\left[r_{0}\ ,\ \vec{r}\right],\ P=\left[p_{0}\ ,\ \vec{p}\right],\ K_{1}+K_{2}=\left[\omega_{\alpha}\ ,\ \vec{k}_{1}+\vec{k}_{2}\right],\ K_{3}=\left[\omega_{\eta}\ ,\ \vec{k}_{3}\right]

(A.143)

We define

\displaystyle J(K^{2})=\int\frac{1}{P^{2}+m^{2}}\frac{1}{(P-K)^{2}+m^{2}}\frac{d^{4}P}{(2\pi)^{4}}

(A.144)

(A.145)

and

\displaystyle G(A,B)=\frac{1}{A^{2}+m^{2}}\frac{1}{B^{2}+m^{2}}

(A.146)

Then

\displaystyle I_{21}=\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{p})}{2\varepsilon_{p}}\sum_{\sigma=\pm 1}\left[\frac{J\left[(P+K_{3})^{2}\right]}{(P-K_{1}-K_{2})^{2}+m^{2}}\right]_{p_{0}=-i\sigma\varepsilon_{p}}

(A.147)

Similarly

\displaystyle I_{22}=\int\frac{d^{3}r}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{r})}{2\varepsilon_{r}}\sum_{\sigma=\pm 1}\left[\frac{J\left[(K_{1}+K_{2}+K_{3}-R)^{2}\right]}{(R-K_{1}-K_{2})^{2}+m^{2}}\right]_{r_{0}=-i\sigma\varepsilon_{r}}

(A.148)

I_{F1}=\int\frac{d^{3}q}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{q})}{2\varepsilon_{q}}\sum_{\sigma=\pm 1}L(R-K_{1}-K_{2},R,R+Q-K_{1}-K_{2}-K_{3})\frac{d^{4}R}{(2\pi)^{4}}_{q_{0}=i\sigma\varepsilon_{q}}

(A.149)

\displaystyle I_{F2}=\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{p})}{2\varepsilon_{p}}\frac{d^{3}q}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{q})}{2\varepsilon_{q}}\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{3}=\pm 1}G(P-K_{1}-K_{2},Q-P-K_{3})|_{p_{0}=i\sigma_{1}\varepsilon_{p}}^{q_{0}=i\sigma_{3}\varepsilon_{q}}

(A.150)

\displaystyle I_{F3}=\int\frac{d^{3}s}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{d^{3}r}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{s})n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{r})}{4\varepsilon_{p}\varepsilon_{s}}\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{3}=\pm 1}G(R-K_{1}-K_{2},S+R-K_{1}-K_{2}-K_{3})|_{s_{0}=i\sigma_{1}\varepsilon_{s}}^{r_{0}=i\sigma_{3}\varepsilon_{r}}

(A.151)

\displaystyle I_{F4}=\int\frac{d^{3}q}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{d^{3}s}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{s})n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{q})}{4\varepsilon_{s}\varepsilon_{q}}\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{3}=\pm 1}G(S+Q-K_{3},Q+S+K_{1}+K_{2}-K_{3})|_{q_{0}=i\sigma_{1}\varepsilon_{q}}^{s_{0}=i\sigma_{3}\varepsilon_{s}}

(A.152)

If we look at the integral, we can find one thing: the first three terms,
I_{11}
,
I_{21}
, and
I_{22}
diverge, the rest becomes finite. i.e.,
I=

+ Finite terms (
2I_{F1}+2I_{F2}+2I_{F3}+I_{F4}

).

\mathcal{K}

Appendix B
Counter term diagrams

B-1
Counter term 1

From

B-3
Counter term 3

From

Appendix C
Renormalization constants

We have from
Kleinert and Schulte-Frohlinde 2001

\displaystyle\gamma(g)

\displaystyle=-Z_{\phi,1}=-\epsilon c_{\phi}

(C.176)

\displaystyle\gamma_{m}(g)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{g}{(4\pi)^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{g^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}+\gamma(g)

(C.177)

\displaystyle\beta(g)

\displaystyle=-\epsilon g+\frac{3g^{2}}{(4\pi)^{2}}-\frac{6g^{3}}{(4\pi)^{4}}+4g\gamma(g)

(C.178)

C-1
Case 1:
K\neq 0

In this case as per
Eq.

64

\displaystyle K^{2}c_{\phi}=-\frac{g^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}\frac{K^{2}}{12}\frac{1}{\epsilon}

(C.179)

Eqs.

C.176
,
C.177
and
C.178
becomes

\displaystyle\gamma(g)

\displaystyle=\frac{g^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{12}

(C.180)

\displaystyle\gamma_{m}(g)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{g}{(4\pi)^{2}}-\frac{5}{12}\frac{g^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}

(C.181)

\displaystyle\beta(g)

\displaystyle=-\epsilon g+\frac{3g^{2}}{(4\pi)^{2}}-\frac{17g^{3}}{3(4\pi)^{4}}

(C.182)

C-2
Case 2:
K=0

Now
Eqs.

C.176
,
C.177
and
C.178
changes because of

\displaystyle K^{2}c_{\phi}=0

(C.183)

So

\displaystyle\gamma(g)

\displaystyle=0

(C.184)

\displaystyle\gamma_{m}(g)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{g}{(4\pi)^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{g^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}

(C.185)

\displaystyle\beta(g)

\displaystyle=-\epsilon g+\frac{3g^{2}}{(4\pi)^{2}}-\frac{6g^{3}}{(4\pi)^{4}}

(C.186)

C-3
Relation

We can relate them as

\displaystyle\gamma(g)_{k=0}

\displaystyle=\gamma(g)_{k\neq 0}-\frac{g^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{12}=0

(C.187)

\displaystyle{\gamma_{m}(g)}_{k=0}

\displaystyle={\gamma_{m}(g)}_{k\neq 0}-\frac{g^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{12}

(C.188)

\displaystyle\beta(g)_{k=0}

\displaystyle=\beta(g)_{k\neq 0}-\frac{1}{3}\frac{g^{3}}{(4\pi)^{4}}

(C.189)

Appendix D
Coupling constant form derivation

We have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Gamma^{\text{diff}}_{n_{k},\vec{k}=0}&=\ \frac{g}{2}\ S_{1}(m,T)-\frac{3g^{2}}{4}\frac{S_{1}(m,T)}{(4\pi)^{2}}\left[\psi(1)+\ln\left(\frac{4\pi\mu^{2}}{m^{2}}\right)\right]\\
&-\frac{g^{2}}{4(4\pi)}S_{0}(m,T)S_{1}(m,T)+\frac{g^{2}m^{2}}{4(4\pi)^{3}}S_{0}(m,T)\left[\psi(2)+\ln\left(\frac{4\pi\mu^{2}}{m^{2}}\right)\right]\\
&-\frac{g^{2}m^{2}}{64\pi^{4}}Y(m,T)-\frac{g^{2}}{32\pi^{2}}S_{1}(m,T)\left[2-\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{3}}\right]\text{.}\end{split}

(D.190)

Let us express

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\text{diff}}_{n_{k},\vec{k}=0}

\displaystyle=gT_{1}+g^{2}T_{2}

(D.191)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\ln\mu}\Gamma^{\text{diff}}_{n_{k},\vec{k}=0}

\displaystyle=gT_{1,\ln\mu}+g^{2}T_{2,\ln\mu}

(D.192)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\ln m}\Gamma^{\text{diff}}_{n_{k},\vec{k}=0}

\displaystyle=gT_{1,\ln m}+g^{2}T_{2,\ln m}

(D.193)

with

\displaystyle S_{N}(m,T)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{m}{2\pi n\beta}\right)^{N}K_{N}(nm\beta)

(D.194)

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}S_{1}(m,T)

(D.195)

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=V_{1}(m,T)+V_{2}(m,T)\ln(\mu)

\displaystyle T_{1,\ln\mu}

\displaystyle=0

(D.196)

\displaystyle T_{1,\ln m}

\displaystyle=-\frac{m^{2}}{4\pi}S_{0}(m,T)

(D.197)

\displaystyle T_{2,\ln\mu}

\displaystyle=V_{2}(m,T)

(D.198)

\displaystyle T_{2,\ln m}

\displaystyle=V_{1,\ln m}+V_{2,\ln m}\ln\mu

(D.199)

\displaystyle\begin{split}V_{1}(m,T)&=\frac{m^{2}}{4(4\pi)^{3}}S_{0}(m,T)\left[\psi(2)+\ln\left(\frac{4\pi}{m^{2}}\right)\right]-\frac{3}{4}\frac{S_{1}(m,T)}{(4\pi)^{2}}\left[\psi(1)+\ln\left(\frac{4\pi}{m^{2}}\right)\right]\\
&-\frac{1}{4(4\pi)}S_{0}(m,T)S_{1}(m,T)-\frac{m^{2}}{64\pi^{4}}Y(m,T)-\frac{S_{1}(m,T)}{32\pi^{2}}\left[2-\frac{\sqrt{3}\pi}{3}\right]\\
\end{split}

(D.200)

V_{2}(m,T)=\left(\frac{m^{2}}{2(4\pi)^{3}}S_{0}(m,T)-\frac{3S_{1}(m,T)}{2(4\pi)^{2}}\right)

(D.201)

V_{2,\ln m}=\frac{4m^{2}S_{0}(m,T)}{(4\pi)^{3}}-\frac{m^{4}S_{-1}(m,T)}{(4\pi)^{4}}

(D.202)

\displaystyle V_{1,\ln m}

\displaystyle=\frac{2m^{2}}{(4\pi)^{3}}S_{0}(m,T)\left[\psi(1)+\ln\left(\frac{4\pi}{m^{2}}\right)\right]-\frac{m^{4}}{2(4\pi)^{4}}S_{-1}(m,T)\left[\psi(2)+\ln\left(\frac{4\pi}{m^{2}}\right)\right]

(D.203)

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{2}\frac{S_{1}(m,T)}{(4\pi)^{2}}+\frac{m^{2}S_{0}^{2}(m,T)}{2(4\pi)^{2}}+\frac{m^{2}}{2(4\pi)^{2}}S_{1}(m,T)S_{-1}(m,T)

\displaystyle+\frac{m^{2}S_{0}(m,T)}{(4\pi)^{3}}\left[2-\frac{\sqrt{3}\pi}{3}\right]-\frac{m^{2}}{32\pi^{4}}Y(m,T)-\frac{m^{4}}{32\pi^{4}}\frac{\partial Y(m,T)}{\partial m^{2}}

Let us define the RGE as

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\ln\mu}=\widehat{RGE}=\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}+\beta(g)\frac{\partial}{\partial g}-n\gamma(g)+\gamma_{m}m\frac{\partial}{\partial m}

(D.204)

with

\displaystyle\beta(g)

\displaystyle=\beta_{2}g^{2}+\beta_{3}g^{3}

(D.205)

\displaystyle\gamma(g)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{2}g^{2}

(D.206)

\displaystyle\gamma_{m}(g)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{m1}g+\gamma_{m2}g^{2}

(D.207)

We have to evaluate

\displaystyle\widehat{RGE}\ \widetilde{\Gamma}\left(m,g,T,\mu\right)\approx_{TLA}0\text{.}

(D.208)

Term by term results are

\displaystyle\beta(g)\frac{\partial}{\partial g}\Gamma^{\text{diff}}_{n_{k},\vec{k}=0}=g^{4}\left\{2\beta_{3}T_{2}\right\}+g^{3}\left\{2\beta_{2}T_{2}+\beta_{3}T_{1}\right\}+g^{2}\left\{\beta_{2}T_{1}\right\}

(D.209)

\displaystyle\gamma_{m}(g)\frac{\partial}{\partial\ln m}\Gamma^{\text{diff}}_{n_{k},\vec{k}=0}=g^{4}\left\{\gamma_{m2}T_{2,\ln m}\right\}+g^{3}\left\{\gamma_{m1}T_{2,\ln m}+\gamma_{m2}T_{1,\ln m}\right\}+g^{2}\left\{\gamma_{m1}T_{1,\ln m}\right\}

(D.210)

\displaystyle-2\gamma(g)\Gamma^{\text{diff}}_{n_{k},\vec{k}=0}=g^{4}\left\{-2\gamma_{2}T_{2}\right\}+g^{3}\left\{-2\gamma_{2}T_{1}\right\}

(D.211)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\ln\mu}\Gamma^{\text{diff}}_{n_{k},\vec{k}=0}=g^{2}T_{2,\ln\mu}+gT_{1,\ln\mu}

(D.212)

\displaystyle\widehat{RGE}\ \Gamma^{\text{diff}}_{n_{k},\vec{k}=0}

\displaystyle=g^{4}\left\{2\left(\beta_{3}-\gamma_{2}\right)T_{2}+\gamma_{m2}T_{2,\ln m}\right\}

(D.213)

\displaystyle+g^{3}\left\{2\beta_{2}T_{2}+(\beta_{3}-2\gamma_{2})T_{1}+\gamma_{m1}T_{2,\ln m}+\gamma_{m2}T_{1,\ln m}\right\}

\displaystyle+g^{2}\left\{\beta_{2}T_{1}+\gamma_{m1}T_{1,\ln m}+T_{2,\ln\mu}\right\}+gT_{1,\ln\mu}

On applying
Eqs.

D.195
,
D.197
,
D.196
and
D.198
on the above equation, it is clear that terms that have coefficient
g
, and
g^{2}
are zero.

\displaystyle\widehat{RGE}\ \Gamma^{\text{diff}}

\displaystyle=0\implies g=\frac{A(m,T)\ln\mu+B(m,T)}{C(m,T)\ln\mu+D(m,T)}

(D.214)

and

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\left[-\gamma_{m1}V_{2,\ln m}-2\beta_{2}V_{2}(m,T)\right]

(D.215)

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=\left[2(\beta_{3}-\gamma_{2})V_{2}+\gamma_{m2}V_{2,\ln m}\right]

(D.216)

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=(2\gamma_{2}-\beta_{3})T_{1}-2\beta_{2}V_{1}(m,T)-\gamma_{m1}V_{1,\ln m}-\gamma_{m2}T_{1,\ln m}

(D.217)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=2(\beta_{3}-\gamma_{2})V_{1}+\gamma_{m2}V_{1,\ln m}

(D.218)

We combine with beta coupling constant relation such as

\frac{d\ g(\mu)}{d\ \ln(\mu)}=\beta_{2}g^{2}+\beta_{3}g^{3}

(D.219)

give rise to the result

\displaystyle\ln(\mu)

\displaystyle=\int^{g}\frac{1}{\beta_{2}t^{2}+\beta_{3}t^{3}}dt=-\frac{1}{\beta_{2}\ g}+\frac{\beta_{3}}{\beta_{2}^{2}}\ln\left(\beta_{3}+\frac{\beta_{2}}{g}\right)+\ln\mu_{0}

(D.220)

Similarly, the corresponding running mass coupling relation is

\frac{d\ \ln(m)}{d\ \ln(\mu)}=\gamma_{m}(g)

(D.221)

Combining with the above

\frac{\partial\ \ln(m)}{\partial g}\frac{dg}{d\ \ln(\mu)}=\gamma_{m}(g)\implies\frac{\partial\ \ln(m)}{\partial g}=\frac{\gamma_{m}(g)}{\beta(g)}

(D.222)

Solving by substituting

\frac{\partial\ \ln(m)}{\partial g}=\frac{\gamma_{m1}+\gamma_{m2}g}{\beta_{2}g+\beta_{3}g^{2}}

(D.223)

\displaystyle\ln\left(\frac{m}{m_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle=\chi_{2}+\frac{\gamma_{m1}}{\beta_{2}}\ln(g)+\left(\frac{\gamma_{m2}}{\beta_{3}}-\frac{\gamma_{m1}}{\beta_{2}}\right)\ln(\beta_{3}\ g+\beta_{2})

(D.224)

\ln\mu_{0}
,
m_{0}
and
\chi_{2}
are the respective integration constants.
Now we have three equations containing mass scale
\mu
, coupling constant
g
,and running mass
m
, so combining
Eqs.

D.224
,
D.220
and
D.214
and solving simultaneously, we get temperature dependent running mass and coupling constant.

D-1
Coupling
g
Limit Case T
\to
0

Here we consider limit case
T\to 0
at
m\neq 0
.
In order to find the coupling nature of
\mu
at T=0,
\beta m\to\infty
, we have to find the rate of convergence of
A(m,T)
,
B(m,T)
,
C(m,T)
,
D(m,T)
as
T\to 0
, because of

g=\frac{A(m,T)\ln\mu+B(m,T)}{C(m,T)\ln\mu+D(m,T)}

(D.225)

Here

\lim_{\beta m\to\infty}S_{N}(m,T)\to 0

(D.226)

Since both numerator and denominator of
Eq.

D.225
contain
S_{N}(m,T)
with varying N. The rate of convergence of the ratios is important. So

\lim_{\beta m\to\infty}\frac{S_{N+1}(m,T)}{S_{N}(m,T)}\to 0

(D.227)

As N increases, the rate of
S_{N}(m,T)
convergence also increases.
The convergence of
Y(m,T)
can be found and is

\displaystyle Y(m,T)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}U(x)U(y)G(x,y)\ dx\ dy

(D.228)

\displaystyle U(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sinh(x)}{\exp\left(\beta m\cosh(x)\right)-1}

(D.229)

\displaystyle G(x,y)

\displaystyle=\ln\left(\frac{1+2\cosh(x-y)}{1+2\cosh(x+y)}\frac{2\cosh(x+y)-1}{2\cosh(x-y)-1}\right)

(D.230)

\displaystyle S_{N}(m,T)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{m}{2\pi j\beta}\right)^{N}K_{N}(j\beta m)

(D.231)

From the expression of
G(x,y)
itself, it is clear that
G(x,y)<\ln(3)
.
So,

\displaystyle Y(m,T)

\displaystyle<\ln(3)\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sinh(x)}{\exp(\beta m\cosh(x))-1}dx\right]^{2}

(D.232)

\displaystyle<\ln(3)\left[\frac{2\pi}{m}S_{\frac{1}{2}}(m,T)\right]^{2}

(D.233)

Therefore

\displaystyle\lim_{\beta m\to\infty}\frac{Y(m,T)}{S_{-1}(m,T)}\to 0

(D.234)

As a result dividing both numerator and denominator by
S_{-1}(m,T)
in
g

\lim_{\beta m\to\infty}g=\lim_{\beta m\to\infty}\frac{\frac{A(m,T)}{S_{-1}(m,T)}\ln\mu+\frac{B(m,T)}{S_{-1}(m,T)}}{\frac{C(m,T)}{S_{-1}(m,T)}\ln\mu+\frac{D(m,T)}{S_{-1}(m,T)}}\approx-\left(\frac{\gamma_{m1}}{\gamma_{m2}}\right)

(D.235)

where

\displaystyle\lim_{\beta m\to\infty}\frac{A(m,T)}{S_{-1}(m,T)}\approx

\displaystyle-\gamma_{m1}\times-\frac{m^{4}}{2(4\pi)^{4}}\left[\psi(2)+\ln\left(\frac{4\pi}{m^{2}}\right)\right]

(D.236)

\displaystyle\lim_{\beta m\to\infty}\frac{B(m,T)}{S_{-1}(m,T)}\approx

\displaystyle-\gamma_{m1}\times-\frac{m^{4}}{(4\pi)^{4}}

(D.237)

\displaystyle\lim_{\beta m\to\infty}\frac{C(m,T)}{S_{-1}(m,T)}\approx

\displaystyle\gamma_{m2}\times-\frac{m^{4}}{2(4\pi)^{4}}\left[\psi(2)+\ln\left(\frac{4\pi}{m^{2}}\right)\right]

(D.238)

\displaystyle\lim_{\beta m\to\infty}\frac{D(m,T)}{S_{-1}(m,T)}\approx

\displaystyle\gamma_{m2}\times-\frac{m^{4}}{(4\pi)^{4}}

(D.239)

Now we have other equations relating
\mu
and
g
as

\displaystyle\ln\left(\frac{\mu}{\mu_{0}}\right)=\frac{-1}{\beta_{2}g}+\frac{\beta_{3}}{\beta_{2}^{2}}\ln\left(\beta_{3}+\frac{\beta_{2}}{g}\right)

(D.240)

When we apply the result
Eq.

D.235
on the above equations, we get

\lim_{\beta m\to\infty}\ln\left(\frac{\mu}{\mu_{0}}\right)=\frac{\gamma_{m2}}{\beta_{2}\gamma_{m1}}+\frac{\beta_{3}}{\beta_{2}^{2}}\ln\left(\beta_{3}-\frac{\gamma_{m2}\beta_{2}}{\gamma_{m1}}\right)

(D.241)

At this approximation, at zero momentum limit, RHS of
Eq.

D.241
goes to a complex number. If we choose
\mu_{0}
in LHS accordingly (i.e., to a complex number/complex function approximation at T=0), one can still make
\mu(T=0)
a real number.

The relation between running mass and coupling relation at
T\to 0
approximated is

\lim_{\beta m\to\infty}\ln\left(\frac{m}{m_{0}}\right)=\chi_{2}+\frac{\gamma_{m1}}{\beta_{2}}\ln\left(\frac{-\gamma_{m1}}{\gamma_{m2}}\right)+\left(\frac{\gamma_{m2}}{\beta_{3}}-\frac{\gamma_{m1}}{\beta_{2}}\right)\ln\left(\beta_{2}-\frac{\gamma_{m1}\beta_{3}}{\gamma_{m2}}\right)

(D.242)

At this approximation one can choose running mass
m(T=0)
, as real or complex by intentionally choosing
\chi_{2}
and
\ln\mu_{0}
accordingly.

D-2
Pressure
P
Limit Case T
\to
0

In Fig. 3, we have chosen different values as
T_{0}
,
P_{0}
,
\chi_{2}
,
\ln\mu
. All those results show a similar course. i.e.,
T\to\infty
,
P\to P_{ideal}
, irrespective of the initial value.

The quasiparticle model derives energy density from standard statistical mechanics at relativistic Bose-Einstein distribution.
i.e.,

\displaystyle<\varepsilon>=\int\frac{\sqrt{p^{2}+m^{2}}}{\exp(\beta\sqrt{p^{2}+m^{2}})-1}\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}

(D.243)

\displaystyle<\varepsilon>

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{p^{2}\sqrt{p^{2}+m^{2}}}{\exp(\beta\sqrt{p^{2}+m^{2}})-1}dp

(D.244)

Put
p=m\sinh x

(D.245)

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{4}}{16\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\cosh(4x)-1}{\exp(\beta m\cosh(x))-1}\ dx

(D.246)

At
T\to 0
, i.e,
\beta m\to\infty
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\beta m\to\infty}<\varepsilon>

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{4}}{16\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\cosh(4x)-1\right)\exp(-\beta m\cosh(x))

(D.247)

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{4}}{16\pi^{2}}\left(K_{4}(\beta m)-K_{0}(\beta m)\right)

(D.248)

We have

\lim_{x\to\infty}K(N,x)\to 0\therefore\lim_{\beta m\to\infty}<\varepsilon>=0

(D.249)

The equation relating pressure with energy is

P(T)=\frac{T}{T_{0}}P_{0}+T\int_{T_{0}}^{T}\frac{\varepsilon(T)}{T^{2}}dT

(D.250)

In
Eq.

D.250
as
T\to T_{0}
, the integral becomes zero. (Integral becomes a zero width integral). So at
T\to T_{0}
,
P\to P_{0}
. One can choose
P_{0}
as negative or positive or zero. We have shown in the figure that irrespective of that value pressure goes to the ideal limit.
In the case of the integrand
\frac{\varepsilon(T)}{T^{2}}
at zero temperature limit,

Assume
m\neq 0
, and
T\to 0\implies\beta m\to\infty
, according to
Eq.

D.248

\lim_{\beta m\to\infty}(\beta m)^{2}K_{N}(\beta m)\to 0

(D.251)

Since energy density at zero temperature limits reaches zero value. There is no point on choosing anything other than zero for
P_{0}
at
T\to 0
. The value of pressure can be made negative at some points if one chooses
P_{0}
as negative at appropriate
T_{0}
. In our case, we found that it reaches the ideal value at a high-temperature limit, whatever may be the initial value.

Appendix E
Matsubara summation results

Here we introduce specific Matsubara summation results involved in calculations we have described earlier, and symbols have the usual meaning. Here
\omega_{n}
denotes the
2\pi nT
where
n
is the Matsubara frequency, and T is the temperature.
\beta
indicates the inverse temperature in natural units, i.e., 1/T.

\displaystyle T\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\omega_{n}^{2}+\varepsilon_{p}^{2}}=\frac{1}{2\varepsilon_{p}}+\frac{n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{p})}{\varepsilon_{p}}

(E.252)

\displaystyle T\sum_{n_{p}=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\omega_{n_{p}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{p}^{2}}\frac{1}{\omega_{n_{p}-n_{q}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}_{q}}=\left[t_{1}(p,q,n_{q})+t_{2}(p,q,n_{q})+t_{2}(q,p,n_{q})\right]

(E.253)

\displaystyle T^{2}\sum_{n_{p}=-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{n_{q}=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\omega_{n_{p}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{p}^{2}}\frac{1}{\omega_{n_{q}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{q}^{2}}\frac{1}{\omega_{n_{p}+n_{q}+n_{r}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{r}^{2}}=S_{1}+S_{2}+S_{3}

(E.254)

\displaystyle T^{2}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{\theta=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\omega_{n}^{2}+\varepsilon_{p}^{2}}\ \frac{1}{\omega_{\theta}^{2}+\varepsilon_{q}^{2}}\ \frac{1}{\omega_{n-\alpha}^{2}+\varepsilon_{r}^{2}}\ \frac{1}{\omega_{n-\theta+\eta}^{2}+\varepsilon_{s}^{2}}=T^{2}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{\theta=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\omega_{n}^{2}+\varepsilon_{p}^{2}}\ \frac{1}{\omega_{\theta}^{2}+\varepsilon_{s}^{2}}\ \frac{1}{\omega_{n-\alpha}^{2}+\varepsilon_{r}^{2}}\ \frac{1}{\omega_{n-\theta+\eta}^{2}+\varepsilon_{q}^{2}}

(E.255)

\displaystyle=\frac{T^{1}_{s2}+T^{2}_{s2}+T^{3}_{s2}}{16\varepsilon_{p}\varepsilon_{r}\varepsilon_{q}\varepsilon_{s}}

(E.256)

with

\displaystyle t_{1}(p,q,n_{r})=\sum_{\sigma=\pm 1}\frac{1}{4\varepsilon_{p}\varepsilon_{q}}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{p}+\varepsilon_{q}+i\sigma\omega_{n_{r}}}\right)

(E.257)

\displaystyle t_{2}(p,q,n_{r})=\sum_{\sigma,\sigma_{1}=\pm 1}\frac{1}{4\varepsilon_{p}\varepsilon_{q}}\frac{1}{\sigma_{1}\varepsilon_{p}+\varepsilon_{q}+i\sigma\omega_{n_{r}}}n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{p})

(E.258)

\displaystyle S_{1}=\frac{1}{8\varepsilon_{p}\varepsilon_{q}\varepsilon_{r}}\left(\sum_{\sigma=\pm 1}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{p}+\varepsilon_{q}+\varepsilon_{r}+i\sigma\omega_{n_{r}}}\right)

(E.260)

\displaystyle S_{2}=\frac{1}{8\epsilon_{p}\epsilon_{q}\epsilon_{r}}\sum_{\sigma,\sigma_{1}=\pm 1}\left(\frac{n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{p})}{\sigma_{1}\varepsilon_{p}+\varepsilon_{q}+\varepsilon_{r}+i\sigma\omega_{n_{r}}}+\frac{n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{q})}{\varepsilon_{p}+\sigma_{1}\varepsilon_{q}+\varepsilon_{r}+i\sigma\omega_{n_{r}}}+\frac{n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{r})}{\varepsilon_{p}+\varepsilon_{q}+\sigma_{1}\varepsilon_{r}+i\sigma\omega_{n_{r}}}\right)

(E.261)

\displaystyle S_{3}=\frac{1}{8\varepsilon_{p}\varepsilon_{q}\varepsilon_{r}}\sum_{\sigma,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}=\pm 1}\left(\frac{n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{q})n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{r})}{\varepsilon_{p}+\sigma_{1}\varepsilon_{q}+\sigma_{2}\varepsilon_{r}+i\sigma\omega_{n_{r}}}+\frac{n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{p})n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{q})}{\sigma_{1}\varepsilon_{p}+\sigma_{2}\varepsilon_{q}+\varepsilon_{r}+i\sigma\omega_{n_{r}}}+\frac{n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{p})n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{r})}{\sigma_{1}\varepsilon_{p}+\varepsilon_{q}+\sigma_{2}\varepsilon_{r}+i\sigma\omega_{n_{r}}}\right)

(E.262)

\displaystyle T_{s2}^{1}=\sum_{\sigma=\pm 1}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{r}+\varepsilon_{p}-i\sigma\omega_{\alpha}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{p}+\varepsilon_{q}+\varepsilon_{s}+i\sigma\omega_{\eta}}+\sum_{\sigma=\pm 1}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{r}+\varepsilon_{p}+i\sigma\omega_{\alpha}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{r}+\varepsilon_{q}+\varepsilon_{s}+i\sigma\omega_{\eta+\alpha}}

(E.263)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ +\sum_{\sigma=\pm 1}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{r}+\varepsilon_{q}+\varepsilon_{s}+i\sigma\omega_{\alpha+\eta}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{p}+\varepsilon_{q}+\varepsilon_{s}+i\sigma\omega_{\eta}}

\displaystyle T_{s2}^{2}=t_{21}+t_{22}+t_{23}+t_{24}+t_{25}+t_{26}+t_{27}+t_{28}

(E.264)

\displaystyle{T_{s2}}^{3}=t_{31}+t_{32}+t_{33}+t_{34}+t_{35}

(E.265)

\displaystyle t_{21}=\sum_{\sigma,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}=\pm 1}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{r}+\sigma_{1}(\varepsilon_{p}-i\sigma\omega_{\alpha})}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{q}+\varepsilon_{s}+\sigma_{2}(\varepsilon_{p}+i\sigma\omega_{\eta})}\ n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{p})

(E.266)

\displaystyle t_{22}=\sum_{\sigma,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}=\pm 1}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{p}+\sigma_{1}(\varepsilon_{r}+i\sigma\omega_{\alpha})}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{q}+\varepsilon_{s}+\sigma_{2}(\varepsilon_{r}+i\sigma\omega_{\eta+\alpha})}\ n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{r})

(E.267)

\displaystyle t_{23}=\sum_{\sigma,\sigma_{2}=\pm 1}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{s}+\varepsilon_{r}+\sigma_{2}\varepsilon_{q}+i\sigma(\omega_{\eta+\alpha})}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{p}+\varepsilon_{s}+\sigma_{2}\varepsilon_{q}+i\sigma\omega_{\eta}}\ n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{q})

(E.268)

\displaystyle t_{24}=\sum_{\sigma,\sigma_{2}=\pm 1}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{r}+\varepsilon_{p}+i\sigma\omega_{\alpha}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{p}+\varepsilon_{s}+\sigma_{2}\varepsilon_{q}-i\sigma\omega_{\eta}}n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{q})

(E.269)

\displaystyle t_{25}=\sum_{\sigma,\sigma_{2}=\pm 1}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{r}+\varepsilon_{p}+i\sigma\omega_{\alpha}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{r}+\varepsilon_{s}+\sigma_{2}\varepsilon_{q}+i\sigma\omega_{\eta+\alpha}}n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{q})

(E.270)

\displaystyle t_{26}=\sum_{\sigma,\sigma_{2}=\pm 1}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{q}+\varepsilon_{r}+\sigma_{2}\varepsilon_{s}+i\sigma\omega_{\eta+\alpha}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{p}+\varepsilon_{q}+\sigma_{2}\varepsilon_{s}+i\sigma\omega_{\eta}}n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{s})

(E.271)

\displaystyle t_{27}=\sum_{\sigma,\sigma_{2}=\pm 1}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{r}+\varepsilon_{p}+i\sigma\omega_{\alpha}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{p}+\varepsilon_{q}+\sigma_{2}\varepsilon_{s}-i\sigma\omega_{\eta}}n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{s})

(E.272)

\displaystyle t_{28}=\sum_{\sigma,\sigma_{2}=\pm 1}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{r}+\varepsilon_{p}+i\sigma\omega_{\alpha}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{r}+\varepsilon_{q}+\sigma_{2}\varepsilon_{s}+i\sigma\omega_{\eta+\alpha}}n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{s})

(E.273)

\displaystyle t_{31}=\sum_{\sigma,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}=\pm 1}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{r}+\sigma_{1}(\varepsilon_{p}-i\sigma\omega_{\alpha})}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{s}+\sigma_{2}\varepsilon_{q}+\sigma_{3}(\varepsilon_{p}+i\sigma\omega_{\eta})}n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{p})n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{q})

(E.274)

\displaystyle t_{32}=\sum_{\sigma,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}=\pm 1}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{r}+\sigma_{1}(\varepsilon_{p}-i\sigma\omega_{\alpha})}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{q}+\sigma_{2}\varepsilon_{s}+\sigma_{3}(\varepsilon_{p}+i\sigma\omega_{\eta})}n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{p})n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{s})

(E.275)

\displaystyle t_{33}=\sum_{\sigma,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}=\pm 1}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{p}+\sigma_{1}(\varepsilon_{r}+i\sigma\omega_{\alpha})}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{q}+\sigma_{2}\varepsilon_{s}+\sigma_{3}(\varepsilon_{r}+i\sigma\omega_{\eta+\alpha})}n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{r})n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{s})

(E.276)

\displaystyle t_{34}=\sum_{\sigma,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}=\pm 1}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{p}+\sigma_{1}(\varepsilon_{r}-i\sigma\omega_{\alpha})}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{s}+\sigma_{2}\varepsilon_{q}+\sigma_{3}(\varepsilon_{r}-i\sigma\omega_{\eta+\alpha})}n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{r})n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{q})

(E.277)

\displaystyle t_{35}=\sum_{\sigma,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}=\pm 1}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{p}+\sigma_{2}\varepsilon_{s}+\sigma_{1}\varepsilon_{q}+i\sigma\omega_{\eta}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{r}+\sigma_{3}(\sigma_{2}\varepsilon_{s}+\sigma_{1}\varepsilon_{q}+i\sigma\omega_{\eta+\alpha})}n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{s})n_{B}(\beta\varepsilon_{q})

(E.278)

\displaystyle n_{B}(x)=(e^{x}-1)^{-1}

(E.279)

\displaystyle\beta=1/T

(E.280)
